### Carrefour

**Identity**

- **Company Name**: Carrefour
- **Headquarters**: Massy (Paris metropolitan area), France
- **Sector**: Retail / Grocery / Consumer Goods
- **Company Stage**: Enterprise
- **Founded Year**: 1959

**AI Implementation**

- **Ai Use Case**: Carrefour deploys AI across its entire value chain in a multi-pronged strategy encompassing: (1) Customer-facing generative AI — 'Hopla', a GPT-4-powered chatbot launched June 2023 on carrefour.fr, assists shoppers with product recommendations based on budget, dietary constraints, and recipe ideas; generative AI also enriches product descriptions for 2,000+ SKUs; (2) Supply chain and demand forecasting — SAS Viya-based predictive models forecast store-level demand daily, reducing out-of-stock by up to 5% and shrinkage by up to 12% for fresh products in French hypermarkets; SymphonyAI GOLD Warehouse Replenishment optimizes inventory across 90 warehouses, raising auto-order accuracy from 20-40% to over 70%; (3) Smart shelf monitoring — partnership with VusionGroup deploys EdgeSense technology combining IoT smart rails, electronic shelf labels, cameras, and computer vision to detect stockouts in real time (reduced from 2 days to 1 hour), with a pilot in Villabe hypermarket featuring 70,000 digital labels, 500 cameras, and 7,000 EdgeSense rails; (4) AI-powered internal assistant — 'ai.carrefour', built on Google Gemini 2.0, rolled out to 125,000 employees across 8 countries by August 2025 to simplify daily tasks, optimize operations, and democratize AI usage; (5) Procurement transformation — Celonis process mining with generative AI reduces procurement quote analysis from 30 minutes to 10 minutes; (6) Retail media and data monetization — Carrefour Links platform leverages 8 billion transactions and 80 million customer profiles to enable hyper-targeted advertising campaigns for brand partners; (7) Bakery production optimization — Google Cloud predictive models determine daily output for in-store bakeries, reducing waste and increasing profitability; (8) Store expansion acceleration — AI-powered market study tools with Artefact reduce initial site analysis from weeks to under 2 minutes.
- **Ai Technology Used**: Multi-vendor stack: OpenAI GPT-4 via Microsoft Azure OpenAI Service (Hopla chatbot, product descriptions, procurement PoC), Google Gemini 2.0 (ai.carrefour employee assistant), Google Cloud BigQuery (Darwin data platform, processing 15,000 SQL queries/month), Google Cloud Recommendations AI (personalization), SAS Viya (demand forecasting), SymphonyAI GOLD (warehouse replenishment), Celonis Process Intelligence with generative AI (procurement), VusionGroup EdgeSense with Captana (computer vision shelf monitoring, IoT smart rails, electronic shelf labels), SAP HANA on Google Cloud (ERP), Artefact AI Factory methodology (cross-functional AI use case development). Infrastructure: Google Cloud Platform for data analytics, VMware Engine migration, and ML workloads; Microsoft Azure for generative AI applications.
- **Build Vs Buy**: Hybrid approach combining strategic partnerships with internal capability building. Carrefour buys/licenses AI capabilities from multiple technology vendors (OpenAI via Microsoft Azure, Google Cloud, SAS, SymphonyAI, Celonis, VusionGroup) rather than building foundation models or core AI infrastructure in-house. Internal teams focus on data platform development (Darwin on BigQuery), AI use case identification, and business integration. Key consulting and implementation partners include Bain & Company (OpenAI integration strategy), Artefact (AI Factory model, data science use cases), Capgemini (SAS deployment), and NTT DATA (Google Cloud migration of 380+ workloads). Carrefour's 'AI Factory' model with Artefact assembles dedicated Feature Teams — each combining a business owner, AI product owner, data engineer, and data scientist — to work on single KPI-focused use cases. This ecosystem approach lets Carrefour move fast without building deep in-house AI research capabilities.
- **Implementation Timeline**: 2018: Strategic partnership with Google Cloud initiated for data platform modernization and cloud migration. 2019: SAS Viya deployed for supply chain demand forecasting after 18-month pilot, making Carrefour the first French retailer using AI in supply chain. 2021-2022: 'Digital Retail Strategy 2026' plan launched with EUR 3 billion investment commitment; Carrefour Links retail media platform scaling; Darwin data platform on BigQuery operational. June 2023: Hopla chatbot launched on carrefour.fr using GPT-4 via Microsoft Azure OpenAI (with Bain & Company); generative AI enrichment of 2,000+ product descriptions; procurement quote analysis PoC with ChatGPT. 2024: Google Data Lab partnership with Artefact scaled across assortment, pricing, supply chain, ecommerce, and marketing; SymphonyAI selected for warehouse replenishment across 90 warehouses; some stores see up to 40% additional revenue on single items from AI-optimized assortment. January 2025: Carrefour Italy deploys SymphonyAI Store Intelligence. April 2025: ai.carrefour platform launched in Spain (3,000 employees initially), powered by Gemini 2.0. June 2025: VusionGroup EdgeSense pilot in Villabe hypermarket (70,000 labels, 500 cameras); Captana camera-based stockout detection already in 35 stores. August 2025: ai.carrefour rollout completed to 125,000 employees across 8 countries. Ongoing 2025-2026: Smart shelf expansion planned for hundreds of additional stores; super-agents (Uno.ai) in development for HR and customer experience.
- **Integration Approach**: Multi-layered platform and API integration strategy. Carrefour does not build a single unified AI system but instead integrates best-of-breed solutions across different operational domains: (1) Cloud-based data platform (Darwin on Google Cloud BigQuery) serves as the central data backbone, unifying analytics across countries and formats; (2) Generative AI applications connect via Microsoft Azure OpenAI APIs (customer-facing chatbot, product enrichment) and Google Gemini APIs (internal employee assistant); (3) Supply chain AI runs as embedded models within SAS Viya and SymphonyAI platforms, integrated with SAP HANA ERP; (4) In-store AI operates through VusionGroup's EdgeSense hardware-software platform (IoT smart rails + cameras + digital labels) as a physical infrastructure layer; (5) Procurement AI embeds Celonis process mining into existing purchasing workflows; (6) Retail media platform (Carrefour Links) integrates Criteo, Google, and LiveRamp for advertising targeting. Google Cloud VMware Engine hosts migrated on-premises workloads (100 legacy applications consolidated). The overall integration philosophy is a federated architecture where different AI tools serve different departments, unified by a shared data platform.

**Business Impact**

- **Before After Comparison**: Before AI: Store managers relied on manual shelf checks and intuition for stock monitoring — stockouts could go undetected for 2+ days. Supply chain forecasting was primarily manual, with only 20-40% of auto-generated procurement orders accurate enough to use without modification. Product descriptions were written manually by merchandising teams. Customer queries on the e-commerce site were handled through rigid FAQ systems and basic search. Procurement teams spent 30+ minutes manually comparing supplier quotes. Market studies for new store locations took weeks of back-and-forth analysis. Store assortment decisions were centralized and uniform across locations. After AI: Stockouts are detected within 1 hour via computer vision cameras on 7,000 smart rails. Over 70% of auto-generated orders are validated without human modification. 2,000+ product descriptions are auto-generated and enriched by GPT-4. Hopla chatbot provides natural-language shopping assistance with budget, dietary, and recipe-aware recommendations. Quote analysis takes 10 minutes. Market studies complete in under 2 minutes. AI personalizes assortment at store level, with some stores gaining 40% additional revenue on individual items. 125,000 employees across 8 countries have access to an AI assistant (ai.carrefour) for daily task optimization.
- **Revenue Model Change**: AI has enabled Carrefour to create a significant new revenue stream through data monetization and retail media. Carrefour Links transforms the company from a pure retailer earning margins on product sales into a media and data platform. By leveraging 80 million customer profiles and 8 billion transactions, Carrefour sells hyper-targeted advertising to brand partners (Mars, Unilever, etc.) through partnerships with Criteo, Google, and LiveRamp — projecting $500 million in retail media ad revenue by 2025 and EUR 200 million additional ROI by 2026. This retail media model mirrors Amazon's highly profitable advertising business but applied to physical retail. Additionally, AI-driven e-commerce improvements support the target of tripling GMV to EUR 10 billion, and smart shelf technology enables in-store digital retail media opportunities (dynamic promotions on electronic shelf labels). The core grocery retail model remains unchanged, but AI adds high-margin digital revenue layers on top.

**Lessons & Risks**

- **Key Success Factor**: Carrefour's single most important success factor is its 'ecosystem partnership' approach — rather than trying to build AI capabilities in-house from scratch (which would be too slow for a traditional retailer), Carrefour systematically partners with best-of-breed technology providers for each use case (Google Cloud for data infrastructure, OpenAI/Microsoft for generative AI, SAS/SymphonyAI for supply chain, VusionGroup for in-store IoT, Artefact for data science, Celonis for procurement) while maintaining internal control over data strategy, use case prioritization, and business integration. The Artefact AI Factory model — with dedicated cross-functional Feature Teams combining business owners and data scientists focused on single KPIs — ensures that AI investments are tied directly to measurable business outcomes rather than becoming abstract technology projects. This pragmatic, results-oriented approach allows a 65-year-old retailer to move at startup speed without the organizational disruption of a massive internal AI build.
- **Main Challenge**: The primary challenge is organizational: deploying AI effectively across a fragmented enterprise of 475,000+ employees in 40+ countries with diverse store formats (hypermarkets, supermarkets, convenience, discount), legacy IT systems, and varying levels of digital maturity. Carrefour had to migrate 380+ workloads and consolidate 100 legacy applications onto Google Cloud before AI could operate at scale. Training 125,000 employees to use AI tools requires massive change management — the 'Tous digital!' initiative trained 60,000 employees in just three weeks, but sustaining adoption and building a true AI-first culture in a traditional retail workforce remains an ongoing challenge. Additionally, the multi-vendor AI stack (Google, Microsoft/OpenAI, SAS, SymphonyAI, Celonis, VusionGroup) creates integration complexity and vendor management overhead.
- **Ethical Or Regulatory Issue**: Carrefour faces several ethical and regulatory considerations: (1) Data privacy — Carrefour Group received a EUR 3.05 million fine in early 2025 for not processing customer opt-out requests properly, highlighting GDPR compliance challenges when handling 80 million customer profiles and 8 billion transactions; (2) EU AI Act lobbying — Carrefour was among 45 European companies that sent an open letter to the European Commission in July 2025 requesting postponed enforcement of the AI Act, citing 'market uncertainty through unclear risk categorisation'; (3) Customer surveillance concerns — VusionGroup's 500 cameras per store and shelf-level behavior tracking raise questions about in-store customer surveillance and consent, even if the stated purpose is inventory management; (4) Job displacement risk — while Carrefour frames AI as augmenting employees (ai.carrefour assistant), automation of procurement orders (70%+ auto-validated), demand forecasting, and shelf monitoring could reduce the need for manual roles in logistics and store operations; (5) Algorithmic pricing — dynamic pricing via electronic shelf labels could raise fairness concerns if prices vary based on real-time demand or customer profiles. Carrefour uses OneTrust for consent management and claims transparency standards beyond GDPR requirements.
- **Lesson For Entrepreneurs**: You do not need to build AI — a traditional enterprise can achieve transformative results by partnering strategically with best-of-breed AI vendors for each use case, while investing heavily in your own data platform and change management to ensure adoption at scale.

**Teaching Relevance**

- **Session Mapping**: S1 (Fundamentals), S3 (Projects), S4 (Business Models)
- **Discussion Question**: Carrefour investit EUR 3 milliards dans sa transformation digitale et deploie plus de 6 solutions d'IA differentes (OpenAI, Google, SAS, SymphonyAI, VusionGroup, Celonis) a travers sa chaine de valeur. En tant qu'entrepreneur, pensez-vous qu'il vaut mieux construire un stack AI integre en interne ou assembler un ecosysteme de partenaires comme Carrefour ? Quels risques et avantages voyez-vous dans chaque approche, en particulier pour une PME qui n'a pas les moyens d'un groupe du CAC 40 ?
- **One Slide Summary**: Carrefour, the world's 8th largest retailer with 15,200 stores and 475,000 employees, launched a EUR 3 billion digital transformation deploying AI across its entire value chain: a GPT-4 chatbot (Hopla) for online shopping, SAS/SymphonyAI for supply chain forecasting (raising auto-order accuracy from 20-40% to 70%+), VusionGroup smart shelves with 500 cameras and 70,000 digital labels for real-time stockout detection, and a Gemini-powered AI assistant now used by 125,000 employees across 8 countries. The standout business innovation is Carrefour Links — a retail media platform monetizing 80 million customer profiles and 8 billion transactions to generate a projected $500 million in advertising revenue, transforming a traditional grocer into a data-driven media platform.

**Strategic & Competitive**

- **Competitive Moat**: Carrefour's AI-driven competitive advantages include: (1) First-party data at European scale — 80 million loyalty customers and 8 billion annual transactions create the largest consumer dataset in European grocery retail, powering both personalization and the Carrefour Links retail media business; (2) Physical-digital hybrid — 90% of sales still occur in-store, giving Carrefour an omnichannel data advantage that pure e-commerce players lack (in-store cameras + digital labels + e-commerce + loyalty data); (3) Retail media network effects — as more brands advertise through Carrefour Links, more data is generated, improving targeting, attracting more advertisers; (4) Scale advantages in AI deployment — the cost of AI tools (Google Cloud, SAS, VusionGroup) amortizes across 15,200+ stores and 40+ countries, creating barriers for smaller competitors; (5) European regulatory familiarity — as a French enterprise deeply embedded in EU regulatory frameworks, Carrefour may be better positioned than US tech-enabled retailers to navigate EU AI Act and GDPR requirements.
- **Open Vs Proprietary Strategy**: Carrefour exclusively uses proprietary and licensed AI solutions — it does not develop or release open-source models. The company licenses GPT-4 through Microsoft Azure, uses Google's proprietary Gemini 2.0, and deploys commercial platforms from SAS, SymphonyAI, Celonis, and VusionGroup. The Darwin data platform is built internally on Google BigQuery (proprietary cloud infrastructure). This fully proprietary approach reflects Carrefour's identity as a technology consumer rather than a technology creator — the company's competitive advantage lies in retail operations, customer data, and physical store networks rather than in AI model development. The risk of this approach is vendor lock-in across multiple technology partners, mitigated somewhat by the multi-vendor strategy that avoids dependency on any single AI provider.
- **Data Strategy**: Carrefour's data strategy centers on its massive proprietary first-party dataset: 80 million loyalty customer profiles, 8 billion annual transactions, and 1 million daily digital platform visits — described as the largest consumer dataset in European grocery retail. The Darwin data platform, built on Google BigQuery, centralizes this data across countries and formats, processing 15,000 SQL queries per month. Data is monetized through Carrefour Links (retail media platform) via partnerships with Criteo, Google, and LiveRamp, enabling brand partners to run targeted campaigns and measure end-to-end impact. In-store data is expanding through VusionGroup's IoT infrastructure (cameras, sensors, digital labels) providing real-time shelf-level and shopper behavior data. Carrefour uses OneTrust for GDPR-compliant consent management and claims voluntary transparency standards beyond legal minimums. The Google Data Lab with Artefact applies this data across use cases: assortment personalization, dynamic pricing, demand forecasting, stockout prevention, fraud optimization, churn reduction, and customer recommendation engines.

## AI in Healthcare & Biotech

### Doctolib

**Identity**

- **Company Name**: Doctolib
- **Headquarters**: Levallois-Perret (Paris), France
- **Sector**: Healthcare / Healthtech / Digital Health Platform
- **Company Stage**: Scale-up (valued at $6.5B, IPO candidate as of 2025-2026)
- **Founded Year**: 2013

**AI Implementation**

- **Ai Use Case**: Doctolib deployed AI across three primary domains: (1) AI Consultation Assistant — launched in October 2024, this is a real-time speech-to-text tool that listens to doctor-patient conversations during medical consultations, transcribes them, and generates a structured medical summary (observations, clinical examinations, diagnosis) in 15 seconds. The system combines three AI components: speech recognition for audio-to-text conversion, LLM-based summarization for creating medical consultation summaries, and medical content codification mapping summaries to standard ontologies like ICD-10. The doctor remains in full control, reviewing, editing, and validating the summary before it enters the patient record. Both audio and transcription are destroyed after the doctor validates the summary. By early 2025, the assistant had been used in 1.6 million consultations, with a target of 10 million by end of 2025. (2) AI Telephone Assistant — acquired through the purchase of Aaron.ai in May 2024, this system answers practice phone calls 24/7, triages patient inquiries (prescriptions, sick notes, appointment scheduling), handles ~80% of routine questions automatically, and routes appointments into Doctolib's system. Priced at EUR 99/month. (3) Alfred — an internal agentic AI system for customer support built with LangGraph, using multiple specialized AI agents with least-privilege access to handle customer requests like calendar access rights management, combining RAG for knowledge base integration with human-in-the-loop confirmation for sensitive actions.
- **Ai Technology Used**: Azure OpenAI Service (GPT models) and Mistral Large on Azure as the core LLMs powering the Consultation Assistant. All models were fine-tuned on proprietary data reannotated in-house, specifically in native French. Typeless (acquired June 2024, EPFL spin-off) provides the speech recognition / voice-to-text engine using LLMs to convert spoken language into structured medical text. Aaron.ai (acquired May 2024) provides the AI telephone assistant technology. For the internal customer support agent (Alfred): LangGraph framework from the LangChain ecosystem for orchestrating multi-agent workflows, RAG architecture, Keycloak for identity management, JWT for service authentication, and Literal.ai for AI evaluation and monitoring. Azure Provisioned Throughput Units (PTU) for flexible model switching. Cloudflare Data Localization Service for ensuring EU data residency.
- **Build Vs Buy**: Hybrid approach combining acquisitions, licensed cloud AI, and in-house development. Doctolib licensed Azure OpenAI Service and Mistral Large on Azure as foundation models, then built extensive custom application layers including fine-tuning on proprietary French medical data, medical ontology codification (ICD-10), and the integration pipeline. The company made two strategic AI acquisitions to accelerate capabilities: Aaron.ai (Berlin, May 2024) for AI telephone assistance, and Typeless (EPFL spin-off, Switzerland, June 2024) for AI speech recognition. The internal agentic customer support system (Alfred) was built fully in-house using open-source frameworks (LangGraph/LangChain). Doctolib spent EUR 115 million on R&D in 2024 (approximately one-third of revenue), with much of it directed toward AI-powered services. The company has a 2,800-person team with active AI/ML hiring.
- **Implementation Timeline**: Early 2024: Doctolib announces AI medical assistant in beta testing with plans for 2024 launch. May 2024: Acquisition of Aaron.ai (Berlin-based AI telephone assistant). June 2024: Acquisition of Typeless (EPFL spin-off, speech recognition). Mid-2024: Beta testing of Consultation Assistant with 350-400 voluntary healthcare professionals across France, running thousands of consultations per week. October 15-17, 2024: Progressive production rollout of Consultation Assistant for general practitioners and pediatricians using Doctolib's clinical software in France. Late 2024: AI telephone assistant priced at EUR 99/month launched in France. November 2025: Full practice management system with AI support launched in Germany. 2026: Planned expansion to additional medical specialties and Germany for the Consultation Assistant.
- **Integration Approach**: Full custom pipeline embedded within Doctolib's existing clinical software platform. The Consultation Assistant is natively integrated into the practice management system — doctors activate it during consultations, it records and transcribes in real-time, generates structured summaries mapped to ICD-10 codes, and feeds validated notes directly into the patient's electronic health record. The AI telephone assistant is integrated into Doctolib's appointment booking system, automatically routing scheduled appointments. The internal Alfred agent system uses an API-based directed graph architecture with LangGraph. All AI features are delivered as part of Doctolib's SaaS subscription platform, not as standalone products. The architecture allows flexible model switching (between Azure OpenAI and Mistral Large) using Azure PTUs without disruption.

**Business Impact**

- **Quantified Roi**: Doctolib reports that the AI Consultation Assistant halves the time doctors spend in front of the computer during consultations, allowing practitioners to spend twice as much time interacting with patients. In a typical 17-minute consultation where doctors previously spent more than a third of the time on the computer, this represents significant time recovery per appointment. The practice management system with AI is estimated to save practices up to 10 hours per week on administrative tasks. The AI telephone assistant saves practices up to 45 hours per week (equivalent to one full-time employee), handles calls 24/7, and automatically manages ~80% of routine patient inquiries. By early 2025, the Consultation Assistant had already supported 1.6 million patient encounters, with a target of 10 million by end of 2025. Doctolib's overall financials show EUR 348M ARR in 2024 (+22.5% YoY), with operating losses reduced 38% to EUR 53.8M (from EUR 87.1M in 2023), on track for profitability in 2025. R&D investment was EUR 115M in 2024, roughly one-third of revenue.
- **Before After Comparison**: BEFORE AI: In a standard 17-minute medical consultation, French doctors spent more than one-third of their time (approximately 6+ minutes) looking at the computer screen — typing notes, filling in records, coding diagnoses. Doctors manually wrote consultation summaries after each patient, adding significant after-hours administrative work. Phone calls went unanswered outside office hours, and staff spent hours daily on routine telephone inquiries (prescriptions, sick notes, scheduling). Practices used separate, fragmented tools for booking, documentation, invoicing, and patient communication. AFTER AI DEPLOYMENT (late 2024-2025): The Consultation Assistant listens to the doctor-patient conversation, transcribes it in real-time, and produces a structured medical summary in 15 seconds. Doctors review and validate (human-in-the-loop), then it integrates directly into the patient record. Computer time during consultations is halved. Doctors report regaining a sense of vocation and reduced mental workload. The AI telephone assistant handles 80% of routine calls 24/7 with no human intervention. The integrated practice management system bundles all clinical and administrative tools into one AI-augmented platform, targeting 10 hours/week saved on admin tasks per practice.
- **Scale Of Deployment**: Doctolib serves 80 million patient accounts and 400,000 healthcare professional subscribers across France, Germany, Italy, and the Netherlands. The AI Consultation Assistant has been used in 1.6 million consultations as of early 2025, targeting 10 million by end of 2025. Beta testing involved 350-400 healthcare professionals. Initial production rollout covers general practitioners, pediatricians, and gynecologists in France, with other specialties and Germany planned for 2026. The AI telephone assistant (via Aaron.ai) serves medical practices primarily in Germany. The platform processes millions of appointment bookings monthly.

**Lessons & Risks**

- **Key Success Factor**: Doctolib's AI success rests on its unwavering commitment to a human-in-the-loop design philosophy and its massive existing installed base. Unlike Klarna's AI-replaces-humans approach, Doctolib designed its AI explicitly to augment doctors, never to replace their clinical judgment. The doctor always reviews, edits, and validates every AI-generated summary before it enters the patient record. Audio and transcriptions are destroyed after validation. This design choice was critical for winning trust in a highly regulated, high-stakes medical environment where physician liability and patient safety are paramount. The second factor is distribution: with 400,000 healthcare professional subscribers already using Doctolib daily, the company can deploy AI features as seamless additions to existing workflows rather than requiring new tool adoption. The combination of trust-preserving design and massive distribution creates a flywheel that competitors cannot easily replicate.
- **Main Challenge**: The biggest challenge has been data privacy and regulatory compliance at the intersection of AI and health data in Europe. In 2021, Doctolib received a BigBrotherAward (German privacy criticism award) for alleged violations of data confidentiality and commercial use of patient data. When Doctolib updated its privacy policy to allow AI model training with anonymized patient data, it faced backlash despite the anonymization and explicit consent requirements. The company must navigate GDPR, EU AI Act high-risk classification for medical AI, and country-specific health data regulations across France, Germany, Italy, and the Netherlands simultaneously. Additional challenges include: ensuring AI accuracy across medical specialties and languages, managing the cultural resistance of some healthcare professionals to AI in clinical settings, and fine-tuning models specifically for French medical terminology when most LLM training data is English-dominant.
- **Ethical Or Regulatory Issue**: Multiple ethical and regulatory dimensions: (1) GDPR and health data privacy — Doctolib received a BigBrotherAward in 2021 in the Health category for alleged privacy violations, with critics arguing the platform collects more patient data than necessary and has confusing/contradictory privacy documentation. (2) AI training on patient data — Doctolib's 2024 privacy policy update allowing AI model training with anonymized data (including voice recordings, medical notes, appointment history) drew scrutiny despite the anonymization and explicit consent requirements. (3) EU AI Act compliance — the Consultation Assistant likely falls under high-risk classification given its medical device-adjacent functionality, requiring risk management systems, data governance, technical documentation, transparency, and human oversight. (4) Data localization — Doctolib uses Cloudflare Data Localization Service to ensure patient data never leaves the EU, and all processing occurs on European servers, but the use of US-based Azure OpenAI models raises questions about data sovereignty. (5) Physician liability — when AI generates medical summaries, questions arise about malpractice liability if an AI-generated summary contains errors that the physician fails to catch during review.
- **Lesson For Entrepreneurs**: In high-stakes regulated sectors like healthcare, design AI to augment human experts rather than replace them — the human-in-the-loop approach builds the trust needed for adoption at scale.

**Teaching Relevance**

- **Session Mapping**: S1 (Fundamentals), S3 (Projects), S4 (Business Models), S5 (Ethics)
- **Discussion Question**: Doctolib a concu son IA pour assister les medecins, jamais pour les remplacer : le medecin valide chaque resume, et les donnees audio sont detruites apres validation. A l'inverse, Klarna a tente de remplacer 700 agents par une IA avant de faire marche arriere. Pourquoi cette approche 'AI augmente le professionnel' fonctionne-t-elle mieux dans la sante ? Est-ce que cette logique s'applique aussi a d'autres secteurs (juridique, finance, education) ? Ou est la limite entre augmentation et remplacement ?
- **One Slide Summary**: Doctolib, the EUR 6.5B French healthtech platform used by 80 million patients and 400,000 doctors across Europe, launched an AI Consultation Assistant in October 2024 that listens to doctor-patient conversations, transcribes them in real-time, and generates structured medical summaries in 15 seconds — halving the time doctors spend on their computer and already used in 1.6 million consultations. Built on Azure OpenAI and Mistral Large, with acquired speech recognition (Typeless) and telephone AI (Aaron.ai), the system is designed so the doctor always validates AI output before it enters the patient record — a textbook example of AI augmenting rather than replacing healthcare professionals, while navigating strict GDPR health data requirements across four European countries.

**Strategic & Competitive**

- **Competitive Moat**: Doctolib's AI moat is exceptionally strong due to three compounding factors: (1) Network effects and installed base — with 80 million patient accounts and 400,000 healthcare professional subscribers, Doctolib has near-monopolistic positioning in France (80% market share for online medical booking). Patients go where doctors are, and doctors go where patients are, creating a classic two-sided marketplace lock-in. (2) Proprietary medical data — the platform processes millions of consultations, generating rich French-language medical data that competitors cannot access. Fine-tuning models on this proprietary, reannotated French medical data creates a quality advantage that generic LLMs cannot match. (3) Regulatory and trust barriers — operating in healthcare requires GDPR compliance, EU health data regulations, data localization, and physician trust. Doctolib has invested years in building this regulatory infrastructure (Cloudflare DLS, European-only servers, HDS certification), creating a barrier that new entrants would take years to replicate. Competitors include DocPlanner, Jameda, and Zocdoc, but none match Doctolib's combination of scale and AI integration in Europe.
- **Open Vs Proprietary Strategy**: Entirely proprietary strategy for production AI systems. Doctolib licenses Azure OpenAI Service and Mistral Large as foundation models, then fine-tunes them on proprietary French medical data reannotated in-house. The speech recognition technology (from acquired Typeless) and telephone AI (from acquired Aaron.ai) are proprietary. The Consultation Assistant pipeline — from audio capture to transcription to medical summarization to ICD-10 codification — is a fully proprietary stack. The internal customer support agent (Alfred) uses open-source frameworks (LangGraph/LangChain) for orchestration but with proprietary logic, tools, and data. Doctolib does not appear to release open-source models or contribute to open-weight medical AI. This proprietary approach aligns with the healthcare sector's requirements for data control, regulatory compliance, and liability management.
- **Data Strategy**: Doctolib's data strategy is built on proprietary clinical data with strict European governance. The platform generates massive amounts of first-party health data from 80 million patient accounts: appointment histories, medical documents, consultation recordings (temporary), and structured medical summaries. For AI training, Doctolib changed its privacy policy in 2024 to allow AI model training using anonymized data with explicit patient consent — this includes search data, appointment history, documents, medical notes, and voice recordings. All AI models are fine-tuned on proprietary data reannotated by in-house medical experts in native French. Data governance is rigorous: patient data is stored exclusively on European servers, Cloudflare Data Localization Service ensures data never leaves the EU, consultation audio and transcriptions are destroyed after doctor validation, and the company holds HDS (Hebergeur de Donnees de Sante) certification required in France. The tension between AI data needs and privacy principles remains an active challenge, as evidenced by privacy criticism following the policy update.

**Regulatory & Impact**

- **Workforce Impact**: Net positive workforce impact in terms of augmentation rather than displacement. Doctolib currently employs approximately 2,800 people and continues to actively hire, particularly in AI/ML, engineering, and data roles. The company's AI strategy is explicitly designed to augment healthcare professionals, not replace them — the Consultation Assistant reduces doctors' administrative burden (halving computer time, saving up to 10 hours/week on admin tasks) while keeping them in full clinical control. The AI telephone assistant effectively replaces the need for one full-time reception employee per practice (saving 45 hours/week), which could theoretically displace medical receptionists, though Doctolib frames this as freeing staff for higher-value patient-facing work. The two AI acquisitions (Aaron.ai and Typeless) brought new engineering talent into the company. At the broader ecosystem level, Doctolib's AI tools help address Europe's healthcare worker shortage by making each doctor more productive rather than replacing physicians.

### Owkin

**Identity**

- **Company Name**: Owkin
- **Headquarters**: Paris, France (with offices in New York and London)
- **Sector**: Healthcare / Biotech / AI Drug Discovery / Diagnostics
- **Company Stage**: Scale-up (unicorn since 2021)
- **Founded Year**: 2016

**AI Implementation**

- **Ai Use Case**: Owkin applies AI across three vertically integrated pillars in healthcare: (1) Drug Discovery — using its proprietary platform (Owkin K, formerly Socrates) to identify novel drug targets and biomarkers from multimodal patient data (histopathology images, genomics, transcriptomics, clinical data), then advancing compounds through AI-optimized clinical trials. The company identified and in-licensed OKN4395, a first-in-class EP2/EP4/DP1 triple inhibitor for solid tumors, which entered Phase I clinical trials in January 2025. (2) Clinical Trial Optimization — partnering with pharma companies (Sanofi, Bristol Myers Squibb, AstraZeneca) to design more precise and efficient trials by using AI to stratify patient populations, predict treatment response, and identify optimal biomarkers, reducing trial duration and improving success rates. (3) AI Diagnostics — developing CE-IVD marked diagnostic tools including MSIntuit CRC (microsatellite instability pre-screening for colorectal cancer from standard H&E slides) and RlapsRisk BC (relapse risk prediction for early breast cancer), which reduce the need for expensive molecular testing. The overarching approach uses federated learning to train models on distributed hospital data without centralizing sensitive patient information, solving the fundamental tension between AI's need for large datasets and healthcare's privacy requirements.
- **Ai Technology Used**: Federated learning (via open-source Substra framework), deep learning on digital pathology (whole-slide image analysis), multimodal data integration (histopathology + genomics + transcriptomics + clinical), OwkinZero biological reasoning model (fine-tuned biological LLM), K Pro agentic AI co-pilot for biopharma, computer vision for cancer subtyping/biomarker discovery. Foundation model research through Bioptimus spin-off (building universal AI foundation model for biology). NVIDIA collaboration for accelerated inference and training. AWS infrastructure for scalable computing.
- **Build Vs Buy**: Hybrid approach. Owkin built its core AI platform, federated learning infrastructure (Substra), and diagnostic models entirely in-house. For drug development, the company in-licensed OKN4395 from Idorsia (exclusive global rights) rather than discovering the molecule from scratch — demonstrating an AI-guided 'smart licensing' approach where the platform identifies promising assets and optimizes their clinical development. Owkin also spun off Bioptimus to build foundation models for biology, maintaining a strategic investment. The Substra federated learning framework was released as open-source, contributing to the broader ecosystem while retaining competitive advantage in its proprietary model training and diagnostic algorithms.
- **Implementation Timeline**: 2016: Founded in Paris by Thomas Clozel (clinical research doctor, son of Actelion founders) and Gilles Wainrib (AI professor). November 2016: $2.1M seed round. 2017-2019: Built federated learning infrastructure, established first hospital partnerships (Institut Curie, Gustave Roussy), launched Substra open-source framework. 2020: MELLODDY consortium — federated learning across 10 pharma companies for drug discovery. November 2021: $180M equity investment from Sanofi + $90M strategic collaboration (4 cancer types), unicorn status ($1B+ valuation). September 2022: CE-IVD mark for MSIntuit CRC and RlapsRisk BC diagnostics. June 2022: Multi-year strategic alliance with Bristol Myers Squibb ($80M upfront + $100M+ milestones) for cardiovascular clinical trials. October 2023: Multi-year Servier partnership (oncology). December 2023: MSD partnership for AI-powered digital pathology diagnostics. February 2024: Bioptimus spin-off raised $35M seed for biology foundation model. May 2024: OKN4395 in-licensed from Idorsia, drug pipeline unveiled. September 2024: AstraZeneca collaboration ($18M initial phase). January 2025: First patient dosed in Phase I OKN4395 trial. February 2025: ATLANTIS program launched (20 hospitals, 7 countries, 11 therapeutic areas). January 2026: K Pro agentic AI co-pilot launched; NVIDIA collaboration for OwkinZero biological reasoning model.
- **Integration Approach**: Full custom pipeline with platform play. Owkin operates at three levels: (1) Federated Research Network — a proprietary infrastructure connecting 35 of the top 100 hospitals in Europe and the US (800+ hospitals in broader network), where AI models travel to the data rather than data traveling to the models; (2) Owkin K / K Pro — an enterprise SaaS platform available on AWS Marketplace that pharma companies and biotechs query through natural language to get biological insights, drug target analysis, and clinical trial design; (3) CE-IVD diagnostic products (MSIntuit CRC, RlapsRisk BC) integrated into hospital digital pathology workflows via partnerships with platforms like Sectra. The company also runs direct drug development programs using its platform to guide clinical decisions. Integration with pharma partners is typically through multi-year strategic collaborations with upfront payments and milestone-based structures.

**Business Impact**

- **Before After Comparison**: Before Owkin: Drug discovery relied on siloed hospital datasets — researchers at one institution could not access or learn from patient data at others due to privacy regulations (GDPR, HIPAA). AI models were trained on small, single-site datasets, limiting their generalizability and statistical power. Pharmaceutical companies ran expensive, broad clinical trials with low success rates (~10% from Phase I to approval). MSI testing for colorectal cancer required molecular testing (PCR/IHC) for every patient, consuming scarce pathology resources. After Owkin: Federated learning enables AI training across 35+ top hospitals without moving patient data, creating models trained on exponentially larger and more diverse datasets. Pharma partners like Sanofi can identify drug targets in 3 months instead of 12+. MSIntuit CRC pre-screens patients from standard H&E slides already being produced, reserving expensive molecular testing only for likely-positive cases. Clinical trials are designed with AI-identified biomarkers to enrich patient populations, reducing trial size and duration while improving success probability. K Pro gives biopharma researchers natural-language access to curated multimodal oncology datasets for real-time biological reasoning.
- **Scale Of Deployment**: Federated research network spans 35 of the top 100 academic hospitals in Europe and the US, with a broader network of 800+ healthcare centers across 7 countries. ATLANTIS program: 20 leading hospitals across 7 countries, mapping 11 therapeutic areas. MOSAIC program: 7,000 patient samples across 7 cancer types with 6 data modalities each (described as 100x larger than any similar multimodal dataset). Active pharma partnerships with Sanofi, Bristol Myers Squibb, AstraZeneca, Servier, and MSD. CE-IVD marked diagnostics deployed in European clinical settings via Sectra integration. OKN4395 Phase I trial (INVOKE) is a global, multicenter study. Approximately 445-475 employees as of early 2026.

**Lessons & Risks**

- **Key Success Factor**: Owkin's single most important success factor is solving the healthcare data access problem through federated learning before it became fashionable. By building the Substra framework and investing years in establishing trust relationships with top academic hospitals (Gustave Roussy, Institut Curie, UPMC Hillman Cancer Center, Charite Berlin), Owkin created a network effect that is nearly impossible for competitors to replicate quickly. The hospitals contribute data access (without sharing the data itself), Owkin's AI generates insights that attract pharma partnerships, the pharma funding sustains hospital collaborations, and the resulting publications in Nature Medicine and peer-reviewed journals attract more hospitals. This virtuous cycle — data network, pharma revenue, academic credibility — is the core flywheel. The founder background (Thomas Clozel as a clinical doctor with biotech dynasty credentials; Gilles Wainrib as an AI professor) gave the company rare credibility on both the medical and technical sides.
- **Main Challenge**: The fundamental challenge is the long time-to-market inherent in healthcare and drug development. Unlike SaaS AI companies that can show quarterly revenue growth, Owkin's drug discovery and diagnostic products face 5-15 year development and approval cycles. OKN4395 only entered Phase I in January 2025 — nine years after founding — and a successful Phase III completion is likely 3-5+ years away. This creates a sustained cash-burn problem requiring continuous fundraising. Additionally, deploying AI diagnostics at scale faces resistance from hospital IT infrastructure fragmentation, regulatory complexity (simultaneous MDR/IVDR and EU AI Act compliance), and the global shortage of digitized pathology slides (most hospitals still use glass slides). Competing with well-funded US players like Tempus ($6.1B market cap, acquired Paige in 2025) and Recursion Pharmaceuticals in a capital-intensive field is also a persistent strategic pressure.
- **Ethical Or Regulatory Issue**: Owkin's federated learning approach was specifically designed to address the central ethical tension in healthcare AI: the need for large, diverse patient datasets versus the imperative to protect patient privacy under GDPR and HIPAA. By keeping data in hospitals and sending models to the data, Owkin sidesteps data transfer risks but still faces regulatory scrutiny around model outputs that could indirectly reveal patient information. MSIntuit CRC and RlapsRisk BC are classified as Class C (IVDR) medical devices, requiring rigorous clinical validation before deployment. Under the EU AI Act, these diagnostic products likely fall under high-risk AI systems (medical devices subject to MDR/IVDR), requiring compliance with risk management, data governance, transparency, human oversight, and conformity assessment obligations. The use of AI in cancer diagnosis raises questions about liability when models make errors — especially given that MSIntuit CRC is explicitly a pre-screening tool that could miss MSI-positive cases if used improperly. Owkin obtained ISO 13485:2016 certification for its AI IVD diagnostic solutions.
- **Lesson For Entrepreneurs**: In deep tech with long time-to-market (biotech, healthcare AI), build your unfair advantage in data access and trust relationships first — the technology will evolve, but a network of 35+ top hospitals trusting you with their data is a moat that takes years to replicate.

**Teaching Relevance**

- **Session Mapping**: S1 (Fundamentals), S3 (Projects), S4 (Business Models), S5 (Ethics)
- **Discussion Question**: Owkin a mis 9 ans et leve plus de $300M avant de doser son premier patient dans un essai clinique. Contrairement aux startups SaaS qui monetisent en mois, la deep tech en sante impose des cycles de 10-15 ans. En tant qu'entrepreneur, comment financeriez-vous une startup deep tech en sante ou les revenus significatifs sont a horizon 10 ans ? Le federated learning d'Owkin — ou les donnees restent dans les hopitaux — est-il un veritable avantage concurrentiel ou une contrainte technique imposee par le RGPD ?
- **One Slide Summary**: Founded in 2016 by a clinical doctor and an AI professor, Owkin solved healthcare AI's biggest bottleneck — accessing hospital data without violating privacy — by pioneering federated learning, where AI models travel to the data across 35+ top hospitals rather than centralizing patient records. This unique data network attracted $180M from Sanofi and $80M from BMS, enabling Owkin to evolve from an AI services company into a drug developer (first compound in Phase I trials, January 2025) and diagnostic maker (CE-marked cancer screening tools) — demonstrating that in deep tech, building trust and data access is the true competitive moat, even if monetization takes a decade.

**Strategic & Competitive**

- **Competitive Moat**: Three-layer moat: (1) Federated hospital network — relationships with 35 of the top 100 academic hospitals in Europe and the US, built over 8+ years of trust, legal agreements, and co-published research. Competitors like Tempus (which acquired Paige in 2025) rely on data centralization, while Owkin's privacy-preserving approach is uniquely suited to European GDPR environments; (2) Multimodal data depth — the MOSAIC and ATLANTIS programs are generating the world's largest curated multimodal oncology datasets (7,000+ patient samples with 6 data modalities per sample), creating a data quality advantage even over competitors with more volume; (3) Pharma validation loop — strategic alliances with three top-20 pharma companies (Sanofi, BMS, AstraZeneca) provide both revenue and clinical validation. Each successful collaboration reinforces credibility with new partners. The open-source Substra framework builds ecosystem trust but the proprietary models trained on the federated network remain Owkin's core IP.
- **Open Vs Proprietary Strategy**: Hybrid open/proprietary. Owkin open-sourced its federated learning framework Substra (available on GitHub), which standardizes how distributed AI training works across institutions — building community adoption and trust while lowering barriers for hospitals to join the network. However, the proprietary AI models trained on this network (PathAIA, OwkinZero biological reasoning model, diagnostic algorithms), the curated multimodal datasets (MOSAIC, ATLANTIS), and the K Pro agentic AI platform are fully proprietary. The Bioptimus spin-off (building a universal biology foundation model) operates independently. This strategy mirrors Red Hat's model: the infrastructure is open, the value-added intelligence is proprietary. Owkin also contributes to open research through Nature Medicine publications and academic collaborations.
- **Data Strategy**: Owkin's data strategy is fundamentally different from competitors: rather than licensing, centralizing, or purchasing patient data, Owkin accesses data in situ through federated learning agreements with hospitals. The data physically never leaves the hospital; Owkin's AI models are sent to each hospital node, trained locally, and only model updates (not patient data) are aggregated. This approach complies with GDPR and HIPAA by design. The MOSAIC program ($50M investment) creates deeply curated multimodal datasets — spatial transcriptomics, single-cell RNA-seq, bulk RNA-seq, whole exome sequencing, digitized H&E stains, and clinical data per patient — across 7 cancer types and 7,000+ patient samples. The ATLANTIS program (launched February 2025) extends this to 20 hospitals across 7 countries in 11 therapeutic areas. Pharma partners can securely upload proprietary datasets to K Pro for analysis. This 'data access without data ownership' model is Owkin's most distinctive strategic choice.

**Regulatory & Impact**

- **Eu Ai Act Risk Category**: High-risk AI system. Owkin's CE-IVD marked diagnostic products (MSIntuit CRC, RlapsRisk BC) qualify as AI systems embedded in medical devices regulated under the EU IVDR, which automatically classifies them as high-risk under EU AI Act Annex I. Compliance requirements include: risk management system, data governance for training/validation/testing datasets, technical documentation, record-keeping, transparency and information to users, human oversight, and accuracy/robustness/cybersecurity standards. Owkin must file a Declaration of Conformity covering both IVDR and EU AI Act obligations. The company's ISO 13485:2016 certification partially addresses quality management requirements. The federated learning approach provides a structural advantage for GDPR compliance (data stays in hospitals), and K Pro as a research tool (not a clinical decision system) likely falls under lower-risk categories. The OKN4395 clinical trial itself is regulated under separate pharmaceutical trial legislation, not the AI Act.
- **Workforce Impact**: Owkin is a net job creator, growing from 2 co-founders in 2016 to approximately 445-475 employees by early 2026, with a multidisciplinary team spanning data science, machine learning, medicine, biology, and regulatory affairs. The company's AI diagnostics are designed to augment pathologists rather than replace them — MSIntuit CRC introduces a pre-screening step that saves pathologist time on negative cases (especially important given the global shortage of pathologists estimated at 5,000+ in the US alone), while keeping the final diagnosis with human experts. In the broader drug discovery workforce, Owkin's platform enables research scientists to work more efficiently (target identification in 3 months vs. 12+) but requires new skills in AI-assisted research. Hospital partners need trained digital pathology staff to digitize slides and integrate AI tools. The Bioptimus spin-off created additional high-skilled AI research positions in Paris.

## AI in Finance, Insurance & Legal

### Alan

**Identity**

- **Company Name**: Alan
- **Headquarters**: Paris, France
- **Sector**: Insurtech / Digital Health Insurance
- **Company Stage**: Scale-up (unicorn since 2021, Series F at $4.5B valuation in September 2024)
- **Founded Year**: 2016

**AI Implementation**

- **Ai Use Case**: Alan deploys AI across four interconnected domains, all built into the product from day one rather than bolted on: (1) Mo, an LLM-based medical conversational assistant — launched November 2024, integrated into the app's chat feature, Mo provides instant health guidance to 700,000+ members. When a member asks a health question, Mo responds immediately, and a licensed doctor reviews and confirms or corrects the AI's answer within 15 minutes. In 81% of conversations, patients chose to interact with Mo even when they had initially come to speak with a doctor. Conversations assisted by Mo received 4.6/5 average satisfaction, slightly exceeding human-only conversations. A peer-reviewed arXiv study ('Conversational Medical AI: Ready for Practice') found 95% of Mo's conversations rated 'good' or 'excellent' by doctors, with zero patient safety concerns; (2) AI-powered claims processing and reimbursement — Alan's Claim Agent uses LLMs to automate investigation of insurance claims. When members submit claims by uploading photos of medical invoices or prescriptions, OCR extracts document details, and the AI agent mirrors human investigative workflows — interpreting the question, reasoning about what additional data is needed, retrieving details from backend systems, and repeating until it has enough information to answer accurately. The initial release fully automated roughly 30% of claims-related support tickets; (3) AI customer support assistant — built initially on GPT-3.5 with extensive scaffolding, the system automates answers to coverage-related questions about health insurance plans. Alan increased automation of customer support from 20% to 40% of requests handled without manual input, with a target of continued expansion; (4) AI-enhanced fraud detection — an in-house AI model flags unusual or suspect claims before payout, and predictive analytics models assist actuaries in spotting trends, setting premiums, and managing risk based on member demographics, health data, and historical patterns.
- **Build Vs Buy**: Primarily build in-house, with selective use of third-party LLMs as foundation models. Alan has been tech-native from founding — co-founder Charles Gorintin (CTO) made engineering a core pillar from day one. The company built its own claims processing pipeline, fraud detection engine, OCR integration, and customer support AI assistant internally. For the LLM layer, Alan licenses external foundation models (started with GPT-3.5, likely uses more advanced models now) but builds extensive custom application layers, scaffolding, and domain-specific logic on top. Mo's medical AI was developed in-house with proprietary safety verification workflows (doctor-in-the-loop). The Claim Agent uses agentic architecture built by Alan's engineering team with tool-calling capabilities connected to their own backend systems. This is a textbook example of building AI into the core product rather than bolting it on — Alan was designed as a digital-first insurer from 2016, meaning AI augments an already fully digital infrastructure rather than retrofitting legacy systems.
- **Implementation Timeline**: 2016: Alan founded by Jean-Charles Samuelian-Werve (CEO) and Charles Gorintin (CTO), becomes first new health insurance licensed in France since 1986. 2016-2022: Company builds fully digital health insurance platform — automated reimbursements, paperless claims, direct doctor chat. 2023 Q1: CTO Charles Gorintin makes AI the main strategic focus area, recognizing LLMs will reshape the business. 2023-2024: Alan builds AI customer support assistant using GPT-3.5 with extensive scaffolding, starting with structured coverage-related questions. Customer support automation rises from 20% to 40%. 2024 February: CEO announces AI is key to reaching profitability, details sales team productivity up 50% with AI tools. September 2024: Series F funding (€173M at €4B valuation), with AI strategy as central pitch. November 2024: Mo AI medical assistant launched to 680,000 members across France, Belgium, and Spain. November 2024: arXiv study published validating Mo's medical accuracy (95% good/excellent, 0 safety concerns). 2025: Claim Agent launched, automating 30% of claims-related tickets. Revenue grows to €505M ARR (48% YoY). Company targets profitability in France by end of 2025, group profitability by 2026. Early 2026: Alan targets 1 million members and €700M ARR.
- **Integration Approach**: Full custom pipeline embedded in a digital-native product. Unlike traditional insurers that bolt AI onto legacy systems, Alan was born digital — so AI is integrated directly into the existing fully digital infrastructure. Mo is seamlessly embedded in Alan's app chat interface, where members already communicate with doctors. The Claim Agent operates within Alan's backend claims management system, using tool-calling to access databases and policy information just as human agents would. Customer support AI is integrated into the same chat interface. OCR processes are embedded in the claims submission workflow (members photograph invoices in-app). Fraud detection runs as an automated pipeline within the reimbursement workflow. The company's asynchronous, written-first engineering culture (GitHub-based decision-making, no meetings) enables rapid iteration on AI features. Alan's platform approach means AI improvements compound across all touchpoints — health guidance, claims, support, and prevention — creating a unified health companion rather than siloed AI tools.

**Business Impact**

- **Quantified Roi**: AI has been central to Alan's path from loss-making to near-profitability: Net loss narrowed from €59M (2023) to €54M (2024), while revenue grew 48% to €505M ARR. The company expects group profitability by 2026 and France profitability by end of 2025. Specific AI-driven metrics: (1) Customer support automation increased from 20% to 40% of requests handled without manual input — directly reducing operational costs while maintaining quality; (2) Sales team productivity increased ~50% through AI-assisted tools; (3) The Claim Agent automates 30% of claims-related support tickets, reducing manual investigation workload; (4) Mo medical assistant achieves 95% doctor-approved conversation quality with 4.6/5 member satisfaction, enabling more scalable health guidance than pure human delivery; (5) Alan plans to hire only 30 additional people (5% workforce increase) while targeting 40%+ revenue growth in 2025, demonstrating extreme operational leverage from AI-enabled scaling. The company serves 700,000 members across 32,000 businesses with roughly 600 employees — a member-to-employee ratio that traditional insurers cannot match.
- **Before After Comparison**: BEFORE (traditional French health insurance, pre-Alan): France's complementary health insurance market was dominated by legacy mutuals and insurers (AXA, Generali, traditional mutuelles). Members dealt with paper forms, opaque pricing, slow reimbursements (days to weeks), phone-based customer support with long wait times, and no digital health guidance. Claims required mailing physical documents. No insurer offered real-time health advice or integrated medical chat. The last new health insurance license had been granted in 1986. AFTER (Alan with AI, 2024-2025): Fully digital experience — members photograph invoices in-app for instant OCR processing and automated reimbursement. AI handles 40% of customer support queries without human intervention (up from 20%). An AI medical assistant (Mo) provides instant health guidance with doctor verification in 15 minutes, chosen by 81% of patients over direct doctor access. AI Claim Agent automates 30% of claims investigation. Fraud detection runs automatically via ML models. Pricing uses predictive analytics rather than legacy actuarial tables. 700,000 members managed by ~600 employees. The transformation is not 'traditional insurer adds AI' but 'AI-native company that happens to be an insurer' — a fundamentally different architecture.
- **Scale Of Deployment**: 700,000 covered members (up from 500,000 in 2023), spanning employees across 32,000 businesses and 20,000 self-employed individuals. Operations in France (primary market), Belgium (via Belfius bank partnership), Spain, and expanding to Canada. Annual recurring revenue of €505M (2024), targeting €700M in 2025 and €1B by 2026. Target of 1 million members by early 2026. Mo AI assistant available to all members via app chat. Customer support AI processes tens of thousands of monthly queries. The Claim Agent handles claims-related tickets across all markets. Alan's AI-powered health walk program (Alan Walk) promotes preventive health across the member base.
- **Revenue Model Change**: AI has not changed Alan's core B2B2C health insurance revenue model — the company sells complementary health insurance plans to businesses (and self-employed individuals) who pay monthly premiums for employee coverage. However, AI is fundamentally changing the cost structure and enabling new product dimensions: (1) Operational leverage — AI automation allows Alan to scale from 500K to 1M+ members without proportional headcount growth (only 30 new hires planned for 40% revenue growth); (2) Health companion platform — Mo transforms Alan from a pure insurer into a health guidance platform, increasing member engagement and retention beyond claims/reimbursement; (3) Preventive health — AI-powered programs like Alan Walk and personalized health recommendations create new value propositions that differentiate from commodity insurance; (4) Marketplace — Alan Shop offers health and wellness products, creating an adjacent revenue stream enabled by member relationships and health data; (5) The Belfius partnership model (bank distributes Alan products) is AI-enabled, as the scalable digital platform allows white-label distribution without per-customer marginal cost. The path to profitability is fundamentally an AI story: AI enables the unit economics that make digital health insurance viable at scale.

**Lessons & Risks**

- **Key Success Factor**: Being AI-native from day one rather than bolting AI onto legacy systems. Alan was founded in 2016 as a fully digital insurer — no paper, no branches, no legacy IT — which meant that when the LLM revolution arrived in 2023, the company had a clean digital infrastructure ready to integrate AI at every layer. This contrasts sharply with traditional insurers (AXA, Generali, mutuelles) that must retrofit AI onto decades-old systems. The second critical factor is Alan's doctor-in-the-loop approach for Mo: rather than deploying medical AI autonomously (high risk in health) or avoiding it (missed opportunity), Alan found the sweet spot — AI provides instant responses, humans verify within 15 minutes, building trust while achieving scale. The published arXiv study validating 95% quality gave scientific credibility that most healthtech competitors lack. The combination of digital-native architecture, responsible AI deployment with human oversight, and rigorous measurement created a virtuous cycle of trust, adoption, and data that competitors cannot easily replicate.
- **Main Challenge**: Regulatory complexity and trust in health AI. Operating at the intersection of insurance (heavily regulated in France by ACPR/Banque de France), healthcare (medical liability, patient safety), and AI (EU AI Act, GDPR) creates a three-way regulatory challenge unique to Alan's position. Getting licensed was itself a barrier — Alan was the first new health insurer in France in 30 years. Building member trust in AI-assisted medical advice required the expensive doctor-in-the-loop verification system (every Mo conversation reviewed within 15 minutes), which limits the immediate cost savings of AI. Additionally, Alan's path to profitability has been slower than initially projected — the company still posted a €54M net loss in 2024 despite €505M revenue, and has had to 'readjust goals' multiple times. Scaling across multiple European markets (France, Belgium, Spain) with different healthcare systems and regulations adds complexity. The company also faces the inherent challenge that health insurance is a capital-intensive, regulated business where moving fast (a startup strength) must be balanced with prudence (an insurance necessity).
- **Ethical Or Regulatory Issue**: Multiple regulatory and ethical dimensions: (1) Health data under GDPR — Alan processes sensitive health data (special category under GDPR Article 9), requiring explicit consent, strict purpose limitation, and robust security. All data is hosted within the EU. (2) EU AI Act classification — AI used for health insurance pricing, risk assessment, and claims decisions is likely classified as HIGH-RISK under Annex III (insurance/creditworthiness), requiring risk management systems, human oversight, transparency, and technical documentation. Mo's medical guidance may also fall under health-related high-risk provisions. (3) Medical liability — when Mo provides health advice (even with doctor verification), questions of liability arise if advice proves harmful. The doctor-in-the-loop system mitigates but does not eliminate this risk. (4) Patient consent and transparency — Alan requires explicit patient consent before Mo engages, and clearly differentiates AI from doctor responses in the interface. (5) CNIL (France's data protection authority) is developing specific guidance for AI in healthcare, jointly with Haute Autorite de Sante, which will directly affect Alan's AI deployments. (6) Algorithmic bias in insurance — AI-driven pricing and risk assessment models could inadvertently discriminate based on demographics, a known concern in insurance AI that regulators are increasingly scrutinizing.
- **Lesson For Entrepreneurs**: Build AI into your product architecture from day one — retrofitting AI onto legacy systems is 10x harder and less effective than designing for it from the start.

**Teaching Relevance**

- **Session Mapping**: S1 (Fundamentals), S3 (Projects), S4 (Business Models)
- **Discussion Question**: Alan a ete concue des le depart comme une assurance 100% digitale — et quand les LLMs sont arrives en 2023, l'entreprise etait prete a les integrer partout. Les mutuelles traditionnelles (AXA, Generali) tentent maintenant de faire la meme chose, mais sur des systemes construits il y a 30 ans. En tant qu'entrepreneur, est-ce qu'il vaut mieux creer une entreprise AI-native dans un secteur regulemente, ou racheter et transformer un acteur existant qui a deja la licence et les clients ?
- **One Slide Summary**: Alan, first new French health insurer licensed in 30 years (2016), was built as a fully digital platform from day one — no paper, no branches, no legacy IT. When LLMs arrived, Alan integrated AI across the entire product: Mo, an AI medical assistant validated by a peer-reviewed arXiv study (95% doctor-approved quality, 0 safety concerns), handles health questions for 700,000 members with doctor verification in 15 minutes; an AI Claim Agent automates 30% of claims investigation; and customer support automation doubled from 20% to 40%. With €505M revenue, $4.5B valuation, and a path to profitability enabled by AI-driven operational leverage (only 30 new hires planned for 40% revenue growth), Alan demonstrates the power of being AI-native in a regulated industry rather than bolting AI onto legacy systems.

**Strategic & Competitive**

- **Competitive Moat**: Alan's competitive moat has several reinforcing layers: (1) Regulatory barrier — being the first new health insurer licensed in France since 1986 means the licensing process itself is a barrier; new entrants face years of regulatory work; (2) Digital-native architecture — unlike AXA or Generali retrofitting AI onto 30-year-old systems, Alan's entire stack was built for digital from 2016, enabling faster and deeper AI integration; (3) Proprietary health data — 700,000 members generating health claims, medical consultations, and interaction data create an increasingly valuable dataset for training and improving AI models for pricing, fraud detection, and health guidance; (4) Doctor-verified AI trust loop — the Mo system with published arXiv validation creates credibility that competitors cannot shortcut; (5) Network effects via B2B2C model — each business customer brings multiple members, and the Belfius bank partnership (distributing Alan products to millions of Belgian employees) extends reach through existing financial relationships; (6) Cultural moat — Alan's unique no-meetings, transparent-salary, GitHub-based engineering culture attracts top technical talent, which is critical for AI development. The combination of regulatory license + digital-native stack + proprietary health data + validated AI creates a multi-layered moat.
- **Data Strategy**: Alan's data strategy leverages its position as a fully digital insurer with no paper trail — every interaction generates structured digital data from day one. Key data assets include: (1) Claims data — every medical invoice, prescription, and reimbursement request processed digitally via OCR, creating a structured dataset of healthcare costs, provider patterns, and member utilization; (2) Medical consultation data — chat-based doctor consultations and Mo AI interactions generate rich conversational health data (with explicit patient consent); (3) Member health profiles — demographics, claims history, preventive health engagement (Alan Walk), and health product purchases (Alan Shop) create longitudinal member profiles; (4) Insurance operational data — pricing, risk, fraud patterns, and actuarial data across 700,000 members and 32,000 businesses. All data is hosted within the EU under strict GDPR compliance. Alan uses this proprietary data to train and improve its fraud detection models, refine actuarial pricing, personalize health recommendations, and enhance Mo's medical guidance. The data flywheel is central to the business model: more members generate more data, which improves AI, which improves member experience, which attracts more members.

**Regulatory & Impact**

- **Eu Ai Act Risk Category**: Mixed classification, with significant high-risk exposure: (1) AI for insurance pricing and risk assessment (actuarial ML models, premium calculation) is classified as HIGH-RISK under Annex III of the EU AI Act, as AI systems intended to evaluate creditworthiness or establish credit scores of natural persons are explicitly listed, and insurance risk assessment falls under similar provisions. Full compliance requirements apply: risk management, data governance, transparency, human oversight, accuracy, and robustness. (2) Mo medical AI assistant likely falls under HIGH-RISK as a health-related AI system, particularly given its role in triaging and advising on medical questions. The doctor-in-the-loop verification system may help satisfy human oversight requirements. (3) Customer support AI and Claim Agent are likely LIMITED-RISK, requiring transparency (users must know they interact with AI). (4) Fraud detection AI is likely HIGH-RISK when it leads to automated decisions affecting claims payout. Alan's position at the intersection of insurance and healthcare means it faces some of the strictest EU AI Act requirements. CNIL and HAS are jointly developing France-specific guidance for healthcare AI that will directly apply. Alan's proactive approach (EU data hosting, consent mechanisms, doctor verification) positions it well for compliance, but full regulatory implementation remains ongoing.
- **Workforce Impact**: Alan demonstrates a unique AI workforce story — rather than replacing existing jobs, AI enables extreme operational leverage. The company employs approximately 600 people (various sources report 550-650) to serve 700,000 members, a ratio that would be impossible for traditional insurers without AI automation. For 2025, Alan plans to hire only 30 additional people (5% workforce growth) while targeting 40%+ revenue growth — meaning AI absorbs the operational scaling. The engineering team is roughly 66 people (approximately 10% of total staff), reflecting the tech-first culture. Alan's no-meetings, transparent-salary, GitHub-based culture specifically attracts AI and engineering talent. There has been no public report of mass layoffs or AI-driven job displacement at Alan — instead, AI has been used to scale the business without proportional hiring. Customer support roles have evolved: the 20% to 40% automation increase means support staff handle fewer routine queries and focus on complex cases. The doctor team remains essential for Mo verification (every conversation reviewed within 15 minutes), creating a new human-AI collaborative role rather than pure displacement.
